{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01EhX2rcQhGPKycjKVGXwuhM","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# cliEval — Architectural Decisions Log\n2\t\n3\t**Status:** 🟡 PROPOSED-R2 2026-05-18 (pending maintainer sign-off)\n4\t**Format:** ADR-lite (one decision per section: Context → Options → Decision → Consequences)\n5\t\n6\t**Revision log:**\n7\t- R1 (2026-05-18): D-1..D-4 initial proposals.\n8\t- R2 (2026-05-18): D-5..D-8 added to resolve the 4 CRITICAL findings from `spec-panel-review.md`.\n9\t\n10\t---\n11\t\n12\t## D-1: PTY layer — fork ptytest vs build minimal pexpect wrapper\n13\t\n14\t### Context\n15\t\n16\tReal Claude Code is a TTY-based interactive REPL. The eval harness needs a PTY (pseudo-terminal) driver that can spawn it, write to its stdin, read from its stdout/stderr, detect \"prompt ready\" states, kill on timeout, and capture exit codes.\n17\t\n18\t### Options\n19\t\n20\t| Option | Pros | Cons | LOC | Risk |\n21\t|---|---|---|---|---|\n22\t| **A. Fork `brandon-fryslie/ptytest`** | Working Claude Code example already exists in repo. Plugin-free standalone class. Active enough (recent commits). | 1 star / 0 forks → bus-factor real. Includes pytest-fixture machinery we don't need. | ~300 LOC adaptation | LOW (ptytest is small, easy to own) |\n23\t| B. Build minimal `pexpect` wrapper from scratch | Clean ownership, no upstream-drift risk, exactly the API we want. | More code to write, debug, test. We re-derive Claude Code TTY quirks. | ~700 LOC fresh | MEDIUM (pexpect has subtle gotchas) |\n24\t| C. Use `pexpect` directly (no wrapper) | Zero abstraction overhead. | Every eval re-implements spawn/wait/expect_prompt patterns; code duplication. | ~0 LOC harness; +~50 LOC per eval | HIGH (drift across evals) |\n25\t\n26\t### Decision: **A — fork ptytest, vendor under `cli/eval/pty/`, fork-and-own posture.**\n27\t\n28\t### Rationale\n29\t\n30\t1. The fork-research report verified ptytest has a working Claude Code example, which means the hardest TTY edge cases (prompt detection, ANSI escape handling, line-buffered reads) are already solved.\n31\t2. Bus-factor risk is mitigated by **vendoring** (we copy the code in; no runtime pip dependency on the upstream repo). If upstream dies, we have a frozen working copy.\n32\t3. The durable dependency is `pexpect` (2.8k stars, active again at v4.9) — ptytest is just a thin layer on top.\n33\t4. 300 LOC adaptation is ~5x cheaper than 700 LOC fresh build.\n34\t5. Option C (raw pexpect) fails the harness-cleanliness goal — evals should look like assertion code, not PTY plumbing.\n35\t\n36\t### Consequences\n37\t\n38\t- We own a small vendored fork forever. `PROVENANCE.md` documents the fork SHA and our diffs.\n39\t- Quarterly review of upstream `pexpect` releases; resync if security-relevant.\n40\t- A new file at `cli/eval/pty/LICENSE` reproducing upstream MIT terms (license compliance).\n41\t\n42\t---\n43\t\n44\t## D-2: Assertion DSL — adopt `Expect.*` port vs raw assertions\n45\t\n46\t### Context\n47\t\n48\tEach eval needs to verify hook side-effects (file presence, JSONL events, settings.json registrations, exit codes, TTY output). The expression of these assertions can be:\n49\t\n50\t### Options\n51\t\n52\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n53\t|---|---|---|\n54\t| **A. Port `mcp-eval`'s `Expect.tools.*` DSL idea as Python primitives** | Declarative YAML manifest possible. Reusable across evals. Single place to add new assertion types. Mental-model match with industry pattern. | +~200 LOC for the DSL itself. Some abstraction overhead (declarative → callable). |\n55\t| B. Raw Python `assert` statements in each eval | Lowest abstraction. No new code. | Each eval is more code. Declarative YAML manifest not possible (or only partial). Failure messages are ad-hoc per eval. |\n56\t| C. Use pytest's assertion machinery directly | Free; idiomatic. | Couples harness to pytest, but the harness is NOT pytest-driven (it's a CLI subcommand). |\n57\t\n58\t### Decision: **A — port `Expect.*` DSL, ~200 LOC, no upstream dependency.**\n59\t\n60\t### Rationale\n61\t\n62\t1. The YAML manifest schema (§5 of design-spec) is a hard requirement to keep eval definitions declarative and reviewable. Raw Python assertions can't be expressed in YAML.\n63\t2. A small DSL (~10 method types: `file.exists`, `file.absent`, `jsonl.contains_event`, `jsonl.event_count`, `settings_json.has_registration`, `exit_code.equals`, `stderr.contains`, `stdout.contains`, `duration.less_than`, `duration.greater_than`) covers all 15 evals comfortably.\n64\t3. The mcp-eval API surface is a proven mental model — we steal the ergonomics without taking a dependency on the framework (which has the wrong architecture for our needs).\n65\t4. Failure messages from a typed DSL are uniformly high-quality (the DSL knows its own structure); ad-hoc assertions tend to produce noisy error output.\n66\t5. The DSL has a programmatic escape hatch for parameterized evals that can't be expressed in YAML — so we don't lock ourselves out of complex assertions.\n67\t\n68\t### Consequences\n69\t\n70\t- `cli/eval/expect.py` is a public API surface; changes need backward-compat care once evals are written against it.\n71\t- The YAML schema (`suites/suite.schema.json`) and the DSL must stay in lockstep — `loader.py` translates declarative → callable.\n72\t- Out-of-scope for v1: LLM-judge assertions (mcp-eval has these; we don't need them for hook validation).\n73\t\n74\t---\n75\t\n76\t## D-3: HOME isolation — extend existing `IsolationLayers` vs separate class\n77\t\n78\t### Context\n79\t\n80\t`src/superclaude/cli/sprint/executor.py:107-182` already implements 4-layer per-subprocess isolation (`CLAUDE_WORK_DIR`, `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`, `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DIR`, `CLAUDE_SETTINGS_DIR`). The eval harness needs a 5th layer: `HOME` override (and `XDG_*` companions). Question: extend the existing class or build a new one?\n81\t\n82\t### Options\n83\t\n84\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n85\t|---|---|---|\n86\t| **A. Compose `IsolationLayers` in a new `HomeIsolation` class** | Reuses 4 existing layers for free. No risk to sprint's existing usage. New `HOME` layer lives in eval code, not sprint code. | Two classes to maintain. Slight indirection. |\n87\t| B. Add `HOME` layer to `IsolationLayers` directly (modify sprint code) | One unified class. Sprint also benefits from HOME isolation (theoretical future use). | Modifies code outside this release's scope. Sprint doesn't currently need HOME isolation, so YAGNI risk. Sprint regression risk. |\n88\t| C. Build `HomeIsolation` standalone (no inheritance/composition) | Maximum decoupling. | Duplicate 4 layers of code. Bug-fixes diverge over time. |\n89\t\n90\t### Decision: **A — `HomeIsolation` COMPOSES `IsolationLayers` (has-a, not is-a).**\n91\t\n92\t### Rationale\n93\t\n94\t1. Composition preserves sprint's existing class verbatim. Zero risk to PR #49-era infrastructure.\n95\t2. The `HOME` layer is conceptually a \"5th layer,\" but its lifecycle (mkdtemp → install hooks → seed state → teardown) is more complex than the existing 4 (which are just env-var settings). Keeping it separate cleanly captures that asymmetry.\n96\t3. If a future sprint feature ever needs HOME isolation, it can either re-use `HomeIsolation` directly or migrate the logic upward — easier path than splitting later.\n97\t4. The interface in `design-spec.md` §7 already reflects this: `HomeIsolation` exposes `.env()` that returns the dict overlay, which the caller merges with `IsolationLayers.env()` output.\n98\t\n99\t### Consequences\n100\t\n101\t- `HomeIsolation` constructor takes an `IsolationLayers` instance as a dependency.\n102\t- Documentation in `cli/eval/isolation.py` cross-references `cli/sprint/executor.py:107-182`.\n103\t- If the sprint `IsolationLayers` API ever changes shape, we have a single integration point to update.\n104\t\n105\t---\n106\t\n107\t## D-4: Eval registry — YAML manifest vs Python-decorator-registered\n108\t\n109\t### Context\n110\t\n111\tThe 15 evals need to be defined somewhere. The orchestrator needs to enumerate them, validate dependencies, schedule them, and report results. Options diverge on where the \"source of truth\" lives.\n112\t\n113\t### Options\n114\t\n115\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n116\t|---|---|---|\n117\t| **A. YAML manifests under `suites/*.yaml`** | Declarative, human-readable, reviewable diffs, schema-validated, easy to add new suites (`fast.yaml`, `nightly.yaml`). Non-Python contributors can author/review evals. | Some Python required for complex pre/post logic; needs an \"escape hatch\" mechanism. |\n118\t| B. Python-decorator-registered (`@eval(id=\"E1\", title=\"...\", requires=[...])`) | Maximum flexibility — eval bodies are first-class Python. Type-checking friendly. | Diff churn higher (Python > YAML in PR review noise). Adding new suites requires more boilerplate. Schema enforcement is via type hints, less ergonomic for non-Python contributors. |\n119\t| C. Hybrid — YAML defines metadata, Python `@assertion_callback` for escape hatch | Best of both worlds for simple+complex evals. | More mechanism to learn; two source-of-truth surfaces. |\n120\t\n121\t### Decision: **A — YAML manifest as primary, with optional Python escape-hatch via `callback:` field for parameterized/complex evals.**\n122\t\n123\t### Rationale\n124\t\n125\t1. **Reviewability** is paramount: a PR adding a new eval should be readable as a YAML diff, not as a Python class with overrides. The 15-eval suite is meant to be a living spec — declarative beats imperative.\n126\t2. The schema (§5 of design-spec) covers the common case (file/JSONL/settings/exit/stderr/duration assertions) elegantly.\n127\t3. The `callback:` field provides escape-hatch power without forcing every eval through it:\n128\t   ```yaml\n129\t   - id: E14\n130\t     title: \"concurrent SessionStart bursts\"\n131\t     callback: superclaude.cli.eval.suites.real_callbacks:E14_concurrent_session_start\n132\t   ```\n133\t   The callback is invoked only when an eval's logic can't be expressed in YAML (E14, possibly E15).\n134\t4. New suites (`fast.yaml`, `regression.yaml`, etc.) drop in as data files with no code changes — exactly the kind of low-friction extension we want.\n135\t5. `loader.py` validates against `suites/suite.schema.json` once; the rest of the pipeline trusts validated input.\n136\t\n137\t### Consequences\n138\t\n139\t- `suites/suite.schema.json` is a public artifact (committed) — schema changes need versioning.\n140\t- Callbacks live in `suites/<suite-name>_callbacks.py` co-located with the YAML.\n141\t- Eval IDs (`E1`, `E2.1`, etc.) are stable identifiers — must not be renamed without a migration note.\n142\t- Parameterized evals (e.g., E2's 3 prefix variants) use a `parameterize:` block in YAML; the loader expands these into separate `EvalSpec` instances at load time.\n143\t\n144\t---\n145\t\n146\t## D-5: Hook-matcher coverage gate — falsifiable definition of \"equivalent regression\"\n147\t\n148\t### Context\n149\t\n150\tGoal G5 in design-spec §1 said \"catch the bug PR #49 fixed and any equivalent future regression\" — but \"equivalent\" was undefined. Spec-panel finding W-1 (CRITICAL) flagged this as unfalsifiable: with no measurable acceptance criterion, the harness has no way to answer \"did I do my job?\"\n151\t\n152\t### Options\n153\t\n154\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n155\t|---|---|---|\n156\t| **A. Coverage gate: every matcher P in `hooks.json` must have ≥1 eval in the loaded suite** | Falsifiable, mechanically enforceable. CI-friendly. Maps to the actual class of bugs PR #49 lived in (matcher↔case-body drift). | Requires `eval doctor --check-coverage`. New eval must accompany every new hook matcher. |\n157\t| B. Snapshot match — assert this commit's matchers equal a frozen golden list | Simple. | Frozen list goes stale; bug-class definition is \"matches a list\" not \"real-world hook fires.\" |\n158\t| C. Leave G5 prose-only (\"intent is clear enough\") | Zero work. | Spec-panel CRITICAL stands; downstream tasklist has no test for G5. |\n159\t\n160\t### Decision: **A — coverage gate enforced by loader + `eval doctor --check-coverage`.**\n161\t\n162\t### Rationale\n163\t\n164\t1. The class of bug PR #49 introduced was \"matcher exists in `hooks.json` but no real-world test exercises it.\" A coverage gate directly inverts that condition.\n165\t2. Mechanical enforcement at load time means the harness fails fast (exit 2) when a new matcher lands without an eval — no silent drift.\n166\t3. The gate runs in two places: (a) `eval doctor` for pre-flight, (b) top of `eval run` for the live run. Both are cheap (single pass over `hooks.json` + manifest).\n167\t\n168\t### Consequences\n169\t\n170\t- `loader.py` reads `src/superclaude/hooks/hooks.json` at run-start, extracts every PostToolUse matcher pattern, and verifies ≥1 eval in the manifest declares `inputs.expect_tool_call` matching each pattern.\n171\t- New hook matcher PRs must include a paired eval — enforced by a CI lint (deferred per non-goals but the harness itself enforces locally).\n172\t- Acceptable escape hatch: manifest declares `coverage_exempt_matchers: [\"<pattern>\"]` with a `reason:` field for matchers intentionally untestable.\n173\t\n174\t---\n175\t\n176\t## D-6: Disk-budget enforcement strategy\n177\t\n178\t### Context\n179\t\n180\tSpec-panel finding N-2 (CRITICAL) flagged internal inconsistency: §14 R4 cited `--max-disk-mb` as the mitigation but §4 flag table omitted the flag. Either add it or strike the mitigation.\n181\t\n182\t### Options\n183\t\n184\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n185\t|---|---|---|\n186\t| **A. Add `--max-disk-mb` flag (default 1024), poll every 5s, abort-on-breach** | Closes the cited risk. Bounded blast radius. Simple implementation. | One more flag. Polling adds tiny CPU overhead. |\n187\t| B. Strike the mitigation from R4; document \"user must monitor disk manually\" | Zero code. | R4 becomes a known accepted risk; eval runs on a constrained box can fill disk. |\n188\t| C. Hard limit via `setrlimit(RLIMIT_FSIZE)` on each subprocess | Kernel enforced. | Per-process limit, not cumulative; doesn't catch the 15-parallel-evals case. |\n189\t\n190\t### Decision: **A — add `--max-disk-mb` flag with 5-second polling.**\n191\t\n192\t### Rationale\n193\t\n194\t1. The risk is real: 15 parallel evals × keep-home-on-failure × TTY transcripts can fill a small disk silently. A polling guard with graceful abort (in-flight evals complete, new ones blocked) preserves results-so-far.\n195\t2. 5-second polling is cheap: one `du -sb` (or Python `os.walk` accumulator) per tick. At 15-parallel, the orchestrator is mostly blocked on pexpect anyway.\n196\t3. Default of 1024 MB matches design-spec §12 sizing (~300 MB total artifacts at 15-eval max-keep). Generous but not unbounded.\n197\t\n198\t### Consequences\n199\t\n200\t- New flag `--max-disk-mb INT` in §4 flag table; default 1024; `0` disables.\n201\t- New orchestrator component `DiskBudgetWatchdog` running in a sidecar thread.\n202\t- New exit-2 reason code: `disk_budget_exceeded`.\n203\t- Summary.json gains `harness.peak_disk_mb` field (also satisfies spec-panel H-1 partial).\n204\t\n205\t---\n206\t\n207\t## D-7: Path-traversal hardening for HomeIsolation\n208\t\n209\t### Context\n210\t\n211\tSpec-panel finding Wh-1 (CRITICAL) demonstrated that a malformed `eval_id` (e.g., `../../../etc/passwd` or `{session_id}` self-reference) could cause `HomeIsolation` to write outside the scratch root. The §14 R7 \"Hard guard\" was prose-only and ambiguous about whether `..` traversal was resolved before the containment check.\n212\t\n213\t### Options\n214\t\n215\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n216\t|---|---|---|\n217\t| **A. Defense-in-depth: loader regex + HomeIsolation `Path.resolve().is_relative_to()` + symlink-resolution after creation + scratch_root allowlist** | Catches the attack at three independent layers. Fails closed at every layer. | More code (~30 LOC). Three checks to maintain. |\n218\t| B. Loader-only regex validation | Simple. | Single layer = single failure point. |\n219\t| C. Trust the manifest author (no validation) | Zero work. | Manifest is data; data ingestion without validation is the original sin of basically every security incident. |\n220\t\n221\t### Decision: **A — three-layer defense-in-depth (regex + Path containment + symlink resolution + allowlist).**\n222\t\n223\t### Rationale\n224\t\n225\t1. The harness operates on user-supplied YAML and writes to disk. This is the textbook injection surface. Single-layer validation is industry-known-insufficient.\n226\t2. Each layer catches a distinct attack class: regex blocks `..` and template tokens; `Path.resolve()` normalizes any survivor; `is_relative_to(scratch_root)` enforces containment; symlink resolution catches the \"scratch dir is a symlink to `$HOME`\" race; allowlist on `scratch_root` itself blocks `--output-dir /home/user/.claude`.\n227\t3. Python 3.10+ (project minimum) provides `Path.is_relative_to()` natively.\n228\t4. The performance cost is negligible (4 stat calls per eval setup).\n229\t\n230\t### Consequences\n231\t\n232\t- `HomeIsolation.setup()` gains a documented pre-flight sequence (regex re-check → resolve → contain → symlink-resolve → write).\n233\t- `EvalConfig` gains an `allowed_scratch_roots: tuple[Path, ...]` field, defaulting to `(/tmp/eval-runs, <repo>/.dev/eval-runs)`.\n234\t- New exception type `HomeContainmentViolation` (subclass of `EvalHarnessError`); maps to status ERRORED.\n235\t- Test `test_isolation.py` MUST include: (a) `..` traversal, (b) absolute-path eval_id, (c) symlink-to-home attack, (d) `--output-dir` outside allowlist.\n236\t\n237\t---\n238\t\n239\t## D-8: Reporter dimensional invariant (consumes N', not K)\n240\t\n241\t### Context\n242\t\n243\tSpec-panel Pipeline-CRITICAL showed §9's summary.json example reported `14 passed, 1 failed, 0 skipped` (totals = 15, the full suite) but the spec text described capability filtering as removing evals from the schedule. If the reporter only sees the K-kept subset, a `--no-mcp` run that drops 5 evals would show \"10 passed\" with no audit trail of the 5 skipped — a silent regression in coverage measurement.\n244\t\n245\t### Options\n246\t\n247\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n248\t|---|---|---|\n249\t| **A. Orchestrator emits N' EvalOutcomes (one per expanded EvalSpec); SKIPPED carries reason; reporter asserts `len(outcomes) == N'`** | Audit trail preserved. Coverage queries answerable. Contract is checkable. | Slightly more state per skipped eval. |\n250\t| B. Reporter takes K outcomes + a separate skipped-list; merges at render time | Same audit trail. | Two channels to keep in sync. More places to drop a skip. |\n251\t| C. Status quo (K only, skip count is metadata) | Less code. | The spec-panel CRITICAL stands; this is the bug being filed. |\n252\t\n253\t### Decision: **A — single list of N' EvalOutcome records; reporter asserts dimensional invariant.**\n254\t\n255\t### Rationale\n256\t\n257\t1. A single dataclass list eliminates the \"two channels in sync\" failure mode of option B.\n258\t2. The contract assertion (`len(outcomes) == counts.expanded_n_prime`) makes the invariant grep-able and test-able; CI can verify it doesn't regress.\n259\t3. Status taxonomy expansion (8 statuses with explicit FAIL/ERRORED/TIMEOUT/INTERRUPTED/SKIPPED/XFAIL/XPASS distinction) is a natural consequence — addresses the spec-panel \"failure-mode taxonomy is sparse\" consensus point.\n260\t4. Performance cost is zero in practice (the SKIPPED records are empty-shell; N' is at most 17 with parameterize expansion).\n261\t\n262\t### Consequences\n263\t\n264\t- New dataclass `EvalOutcome` in `models.py` (see design-spec §9 \"Aggregator data contract\").\n265\t- New status values `ERRORED`, `INTERRUPTED`, `XFAIL`, `XPASS` (in addition to PASS/FAIL/TIMEOUT/SKIPPED) — taxonomy frozen in design-spec §9.\n266\t- New error `ReporterContractViolation` raised on dimensional mismatch at `AggregatedRunReport.from_outcomes()`.\n267\t- Summary.json gains `counts` block (manifest_n, expanded_n_prime, kept_k, skipped_s).\n268\t- Exit-code mapping in §4 cross-references this taxonomy.\n269\t\n270\t---\n271\t\n272\t## Cross-cutting decision: implementation order\n273\t\n274\t(Not a strict ADR, but worth recording.) Per §17 of `design-spec.md`, the implementation phases are:\n275\t\n276\t1. **Phase 1** (1 day): vendored `pty/`, `HomeIsolation`, capability gates, `eval doctor`. Smoke: `superclaude eval doctor` green.\n277\t2. **Phase 2** (1 day): `loader.py`, `models.py`, `expect.py`, `eval describe/list`. Smoke: parse a 1-eval manifest.\n278\t3. **Phase 3** (1 day): `orchestrator.py`, `runner.py`, `reporter.py`, `eval run`. Smoke: 1 real Claude Code session driven end-to-end.\n279\t4. **Phase 4** (0.5 day): wire into `cli/main.py`, Makefile target, .gitignore. Smoke: `make verify-sync` still EXIT=0.\n280\t5. **Phase 5** (1-2 weeks): implement E1-E15 in 3-5 PR batches.\n281\t\n282\t**Rationale for this ordering:** Phase 1 is the riskiest (PTY mechanics). Validating the harness can spawn-and-observe ONE Claude Code session is the gate that determines whether the entire approach is viable. If Phase 1 fails (e.g., Claude Code's TTY behavior breaks pexpect-style detection), we re-pivot before committing to the rest of the architecture.\n283\t\n284\t---\n285\t\n286\t## Sign-off\n287\t\n288\t| Decision | Status | Signed | Date |\n289\t|---|---|---|---|\n290\t| D-1: PTY layer = fork ptytest | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n291\t| D-2: Assertion DSL = Expect.* port | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n292\t| D-3: HOME isolation = compose IsolationLayers | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n293\t| D-4: Eval registry = YAML + callback escape | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n294\t| D-5: Hook-matcher coverage gate (G5 falsifiable) | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n295\t| D-6: `--max-disk-mb` poller (R4 enforcement) | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n296\t| D-7: Three-layer path-traversal hardening | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n297\t| D-8: Reporter consumes N' + status taxonomy | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n298\t\n299\t**Maintainer:** mark each row 🟢 APPROVED + sign + date before invoking `/sc:roadmap`.\n300\t\n301\t**Reject/revise:** add a row to a \"Revisions\" section below; do not edit the original decisions in-place.\n302\t\n303\t---\n304\t\n305\t## Revisions\n306\t\n307\t(none yet)\n308\t\n309\t---\n310\t\n311\t## D-9: Reconcile `validation.status: \"fail\"` label and document manual-triage policy for unclassified deviations\n312\t\n313\t### Context\n314\t\n315\t`.roadmap-state.json:113-117` reports `fidelity_status: \"pass\"` and `validation.status: \"fail\"` (timestamp 2026-05-18T19:47:24Z, six minutes after the final pipeline step completed). However, every pipeline step in `steps.*` — including `spec-fidelity`, `wiring-verification`, `deviation-analysis`, and `remediate` — recorded `status: \"PASS\"`. `spec-fidelity.md:1-7` likewise records 0 HIGH/MEDIUM/LOW deviations and PASS convergence.\n316\t\n317\tThe `validation.status: \"fail\"` label was stamped by a separate validator step that reacts to the 20 records in `spec-deviations.md`. Per `spec-deviations.md:10`, \"deviation classification is not yet implemented. All records currently render as UNCLASSIFIED.\" `remediation-tasklist.md:14-34` then escalates all 20 to `BLOCKING` with owner `UNKNOWN` purely because the classifier never ran — not because spec-fidelity actually regressed.\n318\t\n319\tA manual cross-check (performed during this reconciliation) confirms:\n320\t\n321\t- All 15 spec→roadmap \"orphan\" findings (4 files + 11 `Expect.*` predicate helpers) ARE present in `roadmap.md` — the files appear at `roadmap.md:102,142,409-412` and the 11 helpers are enumerated in row `COMP-010` at `roadmap.md:77`. The analyzer's bare-identifier string match missed qualified references (e.g., `Expect.jsonl.contains_event`) and tabular row contents.\n322\t- All 5 roadmap→spec \"orphan\" IDs (`D-1`, `D12`, `D3`, `D5`, `D6`) are NOT present in `roadmap.md` as bare tokens at all. Only `D-5` and `D-8` (ADR labels) appear (`roadmap.md:57,86,109,344,348`). These are phantom matches from a buggy extraction regex, not real roadmap references.\n323\t\n324\tNet: 20/20 deviations are false-positives produced by the unimplemented classifier. Spec-fidelity is genuinely PASS.\n325\t\n326\t### Options\n327\t\n328\t| Option | Pros | Cons |\n329\t|---|---|---|\n330\t| **A. Add `validation.unclassified_deviations: 20` and `validation.status: \"pass-with-triage\"`; document manual-triage policy here; produce `deviation-triage.md`; resolve `remediation-tasklist.md` per disposition.** | Preserves audit trail. Distinguishes \"fidelity failed\" from \"classifier unimplemented, manual triage performed.\" Unblocks Phase 1 execution. | Requires per-deviation manual evidence (done in `deviation-triage.md`). |\n331\t| B. Flip `validation.status` to `\"pass\"` and delete the deviation records. | Cleanest. | Destroys audit trail of what the analyzer flagged and why the maintainer decided each was a false-positive. |\n332\t| C. Leave the label as `\"fail\"` and block Phase 1 until the classifier is implemented (backlog item `pipeline-classifier-implementation`). | Lets the pipeline self-heal eventually. | Stalls the cliEval release on infrastructure the release itself doesn't need. The classifier is a separate roadmap concern. |\n333\t\n334\t### Decision: **A — pass-with-triage label, manual-triage policy, per-deviation disposition.**\n335\t\n336\t### Rationale\n337\t\n338\t1. The pipeline's 14 step statuses already provide an objective PASS signal; the post-pipeline validator's `\"fail\"` label is one composite indicator that conflates two distinct conditions (fidelity failure vs. unclassified records pending triage). Splitting them removes ambiguity for future readers.\n339\t2. Option B destroys the only artifact (`spec-deviations.md` + `deviation-registry.json`) that would let a future classifier replay the same input and verify it produces the same dispositions. Triage is durable; deletion is not.\n340\t3. Option C blocks an unrelated release on a tooling backlog item with no committed timeline. The cliEval release does not depend on classifier implementation — it only depends on the dispositions being recorded.\n341\t4. Manual-triage policy fits within ADR-lite scope: per-deviation rows with file:line evidence in `deviation-triage.md` are auditable, and `remediation-tasklist.md` updates close the loop with the original BLOCKING entries.\n342\t\n343\t### Manual-Triage Policy\n344\t\n345\tWhen `validation.status` is `\"fail\"` purely due to unclassified-classifier output AND `fidelity_status` is `\"pass\"` AND every pipeline `steps.*` is PASS, a maintainer MAY perform manual triage by:\n346\t\n347\t1. Reading each finding in `spec-deviations.md` plus its registry entry in `deviation-registry.json`.\n348\t2. Cross-referencing the cited symbol/file/ID against `roadmap.md` and `decisions.md` to confirm presence or absence.\n349\t3. Assigning one of the following dispositions:\n350\t   - `NO_ACTION` — finding is a false-positive (analyzer extraction artifact or naming-only mismatch); cite the roadmap.md line that contains the symbol.\n351\t   - `MAPPED → <ROADMAP_ROW_ID>` — finding maps to an existing roadmap row; cite the row.\n352\t   - `ADD_TO_P1` / `ADD_NEW_TASK` — genuine gap; do NOT silently edit phase tasklists; surface for separate gated decision.\n353\t   - `BLOCKING-CONFIRMED` — actual fidelity failure; halts the release.\n354\t4. Recording per-deviation dispositions in `deviation-triage.md` with file:line evidence.\n355\t5. Updating `remediation-tasklist.md` entries (`[x]` + disposition + evidence) and flipping `[UNCLASSIFIED]` markers in `spec-deviations.md` to their resolved classification.\n356\t\n357\t### Consequences\n358\t\n359\t- `.roadmap-state.json` SHOULD gain `validation.unclassified_deviations: 20` and `validation.status: \"pass-with-triage\"` once triage is complete. Per the user's instruction in this pre-execution-gate task, the JSON state file is NOT modified in this turn; the relabel is documented here as the authoritative semantic interpretation and is pending a follow-up edit.\n360\t- Future pipeline runs SHOULD distinguish \"fidelity FAIL\" from \"deviations pending triage\" via separate status fields rather than a single `validation.status` boolean.\n361\t- A backlog item should track implementing the deviation classifier so manual triage is no longer required (see `spec-deviations.md:10` reference to `pipeline-classifier-implementation`).\n362\t- The analyzer's roadmap→spec orphan ID extraction has a latent bug (phantom matches `D-1`, `D12`, `D3`, `D5`, `D6` that do not appear in roadmap.md); the classifier work should fix this regex.\n363\t- Phase-1 execution (`TASK-RF-20260518-cliEval-P1-pty-isolation-gates`) is UNBLOCKED upon triage completion.\n364\t\n365\t\n366\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"85203ef7-94d6-43d8-ad73-4f1edd9e6210","uuid":"360ba77e-98a9-4a4a-bd4b-2a63f845ea80","timestamp":"2026-05-20T07:03:37.771Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/config/workspace/IronClaude/.dev/releases/current/cliEval/decisions.md","content":"# cliEval — Architectural Decisions Log\n\n**Status:** 🟡 PROPOSED-R2 2026-05-18 (pending maintainer sign-off)\n**Format:** ADR-lite (one decision per section: Context → Options → Decision → Consequences)\n\n**Revision log:**\n- R1 (2026-05-18): D-1..D-4 initial proposals.\n- R2 (2026-05-18): D-5..D-8 added to resolve the 4 CRITICAL findings from `spec-panel-review.md`.\n\n---\n\n## D-1: PTY layer — fork ptytest vs build minimal pexpect wrapper\n\n### Context\n\nReal Claude Code is a TTY-based interactive REPL. The eval harness needs a PTY (pseudo-terminal) driver that can spawn it, write to its stdin, read from its stdout/stderr, detect \"prompt ready\" states, kill on timeout, and capture exit codes.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons | LOC | Risk |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| **A. Fork `brandon-fryslie/ptytest`** | Working Claude Code example already exists in repo. Plugin-free standalone class. Active enough (recent commits). | 1 star / 0 forks → bus-factor real. Includes pytest-fixture machinery we don't need. | ~300 LOC adaptation | LOW (ptytest is small, easy to own) |\n| B. Build minimal `pexpect` wrapper from scratch | Clean ownership, no upstream-drift risk, exactly the API we want. | More code to write, debug, test. We re-derive Claude Code TTY quirks. | ~700 LOC fresh | MEDIUM (pexpect has subtle gotchas) |\n| C. Use `pexpect` directly (no wrapper) | Zero abstraction overhead. | Every eval re-implements spawn/wait/expect_prompt patterns; code duplication. | ~0 LOC harness; +~50 LOC per eval | HIGH (drift across evals) |\n\n### Decision: **A — fork ptytest, vendor under `cli/eval/pty/`, fork-and-own posture.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. The fork-research report verified ptytest has a working Claude Code example, which means the hardest TTY edge cases (prompt detection, ANSI escape handling, line-buffered reads) are already solved.\n2. Bus-factor risk is mitigated by **vendoring** (we copy the code in; no runtime pip dependency on the upstream repo). If upstream dies, we have a frozen working copy.\n3. The durable dependency is `pexpect` (2.8k stars, active again at v4.9) — ptytest is just a thin layer on top.\n4. 300 LOC adaptation is ~5x cheaper than 700 LOC fresh build.\n5. Option C (raw pexpect) fails the harness-cleanliness goal — evals should look like assertion code, not PTY plumbing.\n\n### Consequences\n\n- We own a small vendored fork forever. `PROVENANCE.md` documents the fork SHA and our diffs.\n- Quarterly review of upstream `pexpect` releases; resync if security-relevant.\n- A new file at `cli/eval/pty/LICENSE` reproducing upstream MIT terms (license compliance).\n\n---\n\n## D-2: Assertion DSL — adopt `Expect.*` port vs raw assertions\n\n### Context\n\nEach eval needs to verify hook side-effects (file presence, JSONL events, settings.json registrations, exit codes, TTY output). The expression of these assertions can be:\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Port `mcp-eval`'s `Expect.tools.*` DSL idea as Python primitives** | Declarative YAML manifest possible. Reusable across evals. Single place to add new assertion types. Mental-model match with industry pattern. | +~200 LOC for the DSL itself. Some abstraction overhead (declarative → callable). |\n| B. Raw Python `assert` statements in each eval | Lowest abstraction. No new code. | Each eval is more code. Declarative YAML manifest not possible (or only partial). Failure messages are ad-hoc per eval. |\n| C. Use pytest's assertion machinery directly | Free; idiomatic. | Couples harness to pytest, but the harness is NOT pytest-driven (it's a CLI subcommand). |\n\n### Decision: **A — port `Expect.*` DSL, ~200 LOC, no upstream dependency.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. The YAML manifest schema (§5 of design-spec) is a hard requirement to keep eval definitions declarative and reviewable. Raw Python assertions can't be expressed in YAML.\n2. A small DSL (~10 method types: `file.exists`, `file.absent`, `jsonl.contains_event`, `jsonl.event_count`, `settings_json.has_registration`, `exit_code.equals`, `stderr.contains`, `stdout.contains`, `duration.less_than`, `duration.greater_than`) covers all 15 evals comfortably.\n3. The mcp-eval API surface is a proven mental model — we steal the ergonomics without taking a dependency on the framework (which has the wrong architecture for our needs).\n4. Failure messages from a typed DSL are uniformly high-quality (the DSL knows its own structure); ad-hoc assertions tend to produce noisy error output.\n5. The DSL has a programmatic escape hatch for parameterized evals that can't be expressed in YAML — so we don't lock ourselves out of complex assertions.\n\n### Consequences\n\n- `cli/eval/expect.py` is a public API surface; changes need backward-compat care once evals are written against it.\n- The YAML schema (`suites/suite.schema.json`) and the DSL must stay in lockstep — `loader.py` translates declarative → callable.\n- Out-of-scope for v1: LLM-judge assertions (mcp-eval has these; we don't need them for hook validation).\n\n---\n\n## D-3: HOME isolation — extend existing `IsolationLayers` vs separate class\n\n### Context\n\n`src/superclaude/cli/sprint/executor.py:107-182` already implements 4-layer per-subprocess isolation (`CLAUDE_WORK_DIR`, `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`, `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DIR`, `CLAUDE_SETTINGS_DIR`). The eval harness needs a 5th layer: `HOME` override (and `XDG_*` companions). Question: extend the existing class or build a new one?\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Compose `IsolationLayers` in a new `HomeIsolation` class** | Reuses 4 existing layers for free. No risk to sprint's existing usage. New `HOME` layer lives in eval code, not sprint code. | Two classes to maintain. Slight indirection. |\n| B. Add `HOME` layer to `IsolationLayers` directly (modify sprint code) | One unified class. Sprint also benefits from HOME isolation (theoretical future use). | Modifies code outside this release's scope. Sprint doesn't currently need HOME isolation, so YAGNI risk. Sprint regression risk. |\n| C. Build `HomeIsolation` standalone (no inheritance/composition) | Maximum decoupling. | Duplicate 4 layers of code. Bug-fixes diverge over time. |\n\n### Decision: **A — `HomeIsolation` COMPOSES `IsolationLayers` (has-a, not is-a).**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. Composition preserves sprint's existing class verbatim. Zero risk to PR #49-era infrastructure.\n2. The `HOME` layer is conceptually a \"5th layer,\" but its lifecycle (mkdtemp → install hooks → seed state → teardown) is more complex than the existing 4 (which are just env-var settings). Keeping it separate cleanly captures that asymmetry.\n3. If a future sprint feature ever needs HOME isolation, it can either re-use `HomeIsolation` directly or migrate the logic upward — easier path than splitting later.\n4. The interface in `design-spec.md` §7 already reflects this: `HomeIsolation` exposes `.env()` that returns the dict overlay, which the caller merges with `IsolationLayers.env()` output.\n\n### Consequences\n\n- `HomeIsolation` constructor takes an `IsolationLayers` instance as a dependency.\n- Documentation in `cli/eval/isolation.py` cross-references `cli/sprint/executor.py:107-182`.\n- If the sprint `IsolationLayers` API ever changes shape, we have a single integration point to update.\n\n---\n\n## D-4: Eval registry — YAML manifest vs Python-decorator-registered\n\n### Context\n\nThe 15 evals need to be defined somewhere. The orchestrator needs to enumerate them, validate dependencies, schedule them, and report results. Options diverge on where the \"source of truth\" lives.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. YAML manifests under `suites/*.yaml`** | Declarative, human-readable, reviewable diffs, schema-validated, easy to add new suites (`fast.yaml`, `nightly.yaml`). Non-Python contributors can author/review evals. | Some Python required for complex pre/post logic; needs an \"escape hatch\" mechanism. |\n| B. Python-decorator-registered (`@eval(id=\"E1\", title=\"...\", requires=[...])`) | Maximum flexibility — eval bodies are first-class Python. Type-checking friendly. | Diff churn higher (Python > YAML in PR review noise). Adding new suites requires more boilerplate. Schema enforcement is via type hints, less ergonomic for non-Python contributors. |\n| C. Hybrid — YAML defines metadata, Python `@assertion_callback` for escape hatch | Best of both worlds for simple+complex evals. | More mechanism to learn; two source-of-truth surfaces. |\n\n### Decision: **A — YAML manifest as primary, with optional Python escape-hatch via `callback:` field for parameterized/complex evals.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. **Reviewability** is paramount: a PR adding a new eval should be readable as a YAML diff, not as a Python class with overrides. The 15-eval suite is meant to be a living spec — declarative beats imperative.\n2. The schema (§5 of design-spec) covers the common case (file/JSONL/settings/exit/stderr/duration assertions) elegantly.\n3. The `callback:` field provides escape-hatch power without forcing every eval through it:\n   ```yaml\n   - id: E14\n     title: \"concurrent SessionStart bursts\"\n     callback: superclaude.cli.eval.suites.real_callbacks:E14_concurrent_session_start\n   ```\n   The callback is invoked only when an eval's logic can't be expressed in YAML (E14, possibly E15).\n4. New suites (`fast.yaml`, `regression.yaml`, etc.) drop in as data files with no code changes — exactly the kind of low-friction extension we want.\n5. `loader.py` validates against `suites/suite.schema.json` once; the rest of the pipeline trusts validated input.\n\n### Consequences\n\n- `suites/suite.schema.json` is a public artifact (committed) — schema changes need versioning.\n- Callbacks live in `suites/<suite-name>_callbacks.py` co-located with the YAML.\n- Eval IDs (`E1`, `E2.1`, etc.) are stable identifiers — must not be renamed without a migration note.\n- Parameterized evals (e.g., E2's 3 prefix variants) use a `parameterize:` block in YAML; the loader expands these into separate `EvalSpec` instances at load time.\n\n---\n\n## D-5: Hook-matcher coverage gate — falsifiable definition of \"equivalent regression\"\n\n### Context\n\nGoal G5 in design-spec §1 said \"catch the bug PR #49 fixed and any equivalent future regression\" — but \"equivalent\" was undefined. Spec-panel finding W-1 (CRITICAL) flagged this as unfalsifiable: with no measurable acceptance criterion, the harness has no way to answer \"did I do my job?\"\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Coverage gate: every matcher P in `hooks.json` must have ≥1 eval in the loaded suite** | Falsifiable, mechanically enforceable. CI-friendly. Maps to the actual class of bugs PR #49 lived in (matcher↔case-body drift). | Requires `eval doctor --check-coverage`. New eval must accompany every new hook matcher. |\n| B. Snapshot match — assert this commit's matchers equal a frozen golden list | Simple. | Frozen list goes stale; bug-class definition is \"matches a list\" not \"real-world hook fires.\" |\n| C. Leave G5 prose-only (\"intent is clear enough\") | Zero work. | Spec-panel CRITICAL stands; downstream tasklist has no test for G5. |\n\n### Decision: **A — coverage gate enforced by loader + `eval doctor --check-coverage`.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. The class of bug PR #49 introduced was \"matcher exists in `hooks.json` but no real-world test exercises it.\" A coverage gate directly inverts that condition.\n2. Mechanical enforcement at load time means the harness fails fast (exit 2) when a new matcher lands without an eval — no silent drift.\n3. The gate runs in two places: (a) `eval doctor` for pre-flight, (b) top of `eval run` for the live run. Both are cheap (single pass over `hooks.json` + manifest).\n\n### Consequences\n\n- `loader.py` reads `src/superclaude/hooks/hooks.json` at run-start, extracts every PostToolUse matcher pattern, and verifies ≥1 eval in the manifest declares `inputs.expect_tool_call` matching each pattern.\n- New hook matcher PRs must include a paired eval — enforced by a CI lint (deferred per non-goals but the harness itself enforces locally).\n- Acceptable escape hatch: manifest declares `coverage_exempt_matchers: [\"<pattern>\"]` with a `reason:` field for matchers intentionally untestable.\n\n---\n\n## D-6: Disk-budget enforcement strategy\n\n### Context\n\nSpec-panel finding N-2 (CRITICAL) flagged internal inconsistency: §14 R4 cited `--max-disk-mb` as the mitigation but §4 flag table omitted the flag. Either add it or strike the mitigation.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Add `--max-disk-mb` flag (default 1024), poll every 5s, abort-on-breach** | Closes the cited risk. Bounded blast radius. Simple implementation. | One more flag. Polling adds tiny CPU overhead. |\n| B. Strike the mitigation from R4; document \"user must monitor disk manually\" | Zero code. | R4 becomes a known accepted risk; eval runs on a constrained box can fill disk. |\n| C. Hard limit via `setrlimit(RLIMIT_FSIZE)` on each subprocess | Kernel enforced. | Per-process limit, not cumulative; doesn't catch the 15-parallel-evals case. |\n\n### Decision: **A — add `--max-disk-mb` flag with 5-second polling.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. The risk is real: 15 parallel evals × keep-home-on-failure × TTY transcripts can fill a small disk silently. A polling guard with graceful abort (in-flight evals complete, new ones blocked) preserves results-so-far.\n2. 5-second polling is cheap: one `du -sb` (or Python `os.walk` accumulator) per tick. At 15-parallel, the orchestrator is mostly blocked on pexpect anyway.\n3. Default of 1024 MB matches design-spec §12 sizing (~300 MB total artifacts at 15-eval max-keep). Generous but not unbounded.\n\n### Consequences\n\n- New flag `--max-disk-mb INT` in §4 flag table; default 1024; `0` disables.\n- New orchestrator component `DiskBudgetWatchdog` running in a sidecar thread.\n- New exit-2 reason code: `disk_budget_exceeded`.\n- Summary.json gains `harness.peak_disk_mb` field (also satisfies spec-panel H-1 partial).\n\n---\n\n## D-7: Path-traversal hardening for HomeIsolation\n\n### Context\n\nSpec-panel finding Wh-1 (CRITICAL) demonstrated that a malformed `eval_id` (e.g., `../../../etc/passwd` or `{session_id}` self-reference) could cause `HomeIsolation` to write outside the scratch root. The §14 R7 \"Hard guard\" was prose-only and ambiguous about whether `..` traversal was resolved before the containment check.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Defense-in-depth: loader regex + HomeIsolation `Path.resolve().is_relative_to()` + symlink-resolution after creation + scratch_root allowlist** | Catches the attack at three independent layers. Fails closed at every layer. | More code (~30 LOC). Three checks to maintain. |\n| B. Loader-only regex validation | Simple. | Single layer = single failure point. |\n| C. Trust the manifest author (no validation) | Zero work. | Manifest is data; data ingestion without validation is the original sin of basically every security incident. |\n\n### Decision: **A — three-layer defense-in-depth (regex + Path containment + symlink resolution + allowlist).**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. The harness operates on user-supplied YAML and writes to disk. This is the textbook injection surface. Single-layer validation is industry-known-insufficient.\n2. Each layer catches a distinct attack class: regex blocks `..` and template tokens; `Path.resolve()` normalizes any survivor; `is_relative_to(scratch_root)` enforces containment; symlink resolution catches the \"scratch dir is a symlink to `$HOME`\" race; allowlist on `scratch_root` itself blocks `--output-dir /home/user/.claude`.\n3. Python 3.10+ (project minimum) provides `Path.is_relative_to()` natively.\n4. The performance cost is negligible (4 stat calls per eval setup).\n\n### Consequences\n\n- `HomeIsolation.setup()` gains a documented pre-flight sequence (regex re-check → resolve → contain → symlink-resolve → write).\n- `EvalConfig` gains an `allowed_scratch_roots: tuple[Path, ...]` field, defaulting to `(/tmp/eval-runs, <repo>/.dev/eval-runs)`.\n- New exception type `HomeContainmentViolation` (subclass of `EvalHarnessError`); maps to status ERRORED.\n- Test `test_isolation.py` MUST include: (a) `..` traversal, (b) absolute-path eval_id, (c) symlink-to-home attack, (d) `--output-dir` outside allowlist.\n\n---\n\n## D-8: Reporter dimensional invariant (consumes N', not K)\n\n### Context\n\nSpec-panel Pipeline-CRITICAL showed §9's summary.json example reported `14 passed, 1 failed, 0 skipped` (totals = 15, the full suite) but the spec text described capability filtering as removing evals from the schedule. If the reporter only sees the K-kept subset, a `--no-mcp` run that drops 5 evals would show \"10 passed\" with no audit trail of the 5 skipped — a silent regression in coverage measurement.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Orchestrator emits N' EvalOutcomes (one per expanded EvalSpec); SKIPPED carries reason; reporter asserts `len(outcomes) == N'`** | Audit trail preserved. Coverage queries answerable. Contract is checkable. | Slightly more state per skipped eval. |\n| B. Reporter takes K outcomes + a separate skipped-list; merges at render time | Same audit trail. | Two channels to keep in sync. More places to drop a skip. |\n| C. Status quo (K only, skip count is metadata) | Less code. | The spec-panel CRITICAL stands; this is the bug being filed. |\n\n### Decision: **A — single list of N' EvalOutcome records; reporter asserts dimensional invariant.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. A single dataclass list eliminates the \"two channels in sync\" failure mode of option B.\n2. The contract assertion (`len(outcomes) == counts.expanded_n_prime`) makes the invariant grep-able and test-able; CI can verify it doesn't regress.\n3. Status taxonomy expansion (8 statuses with explicit FAIL/ERRORED/TIMEOUT/INTERRUPTED/SKIPPED/XFAIL/XPASS distinction) is a natural consequence — addresses the spec-panel \"failure-mode taxonomy is sparse\" consensus point.\n4. Performance cost is zero in practice (the SKIPPED records are empty-shell; N' is at most 17 with parameterize expansion).\n\n### Consequences\n\n- New dataclass `EvalOutcome` in `models.py` (see design-spec §9 \"Aggregator data contract\").\n- New status values `ERRORED`, `INTERRUPTED`, `XFAIL`, `XPASS` (in addition to PASS/FAIL/TIMEOUT/SKIPPED) — taxonomy frozen in design-spec §9.\n- New error `ReporterContractViolation` raised on dimensional mismatch at `AggregatedRunReport.from_outcomes()`.\n- Summary.json gains `counts` block (manifest_n, expanded_n_prime, kept_k, skipped_s).\n- Exit-code mapping in §4 cross-references this taxonomy.\n\n---\n\n## Cross-cutting decision: implementation order\n\n(Not a strict ADR, but worth recording.) Per §17 of `design-spec.md`, the implementation phases are:\n\n1. **Phase 1** (1 day): vendored `pty/`, `HomeIsolation`, capability gates, `eval doctor`. Smoke: `superclaude eval doctor` green.\n2. **Phase 2** (1 day): `loader.py`, `models.py`, `expect.py`, `eval describe/list`. Smoke: parse a 1-eval manifest.\n3. **Phase 3** (1 day): `orchestrator.py`, `runner.py`, `reporter.py`, `eval run`. Smoke: 1 real Claude Code session driven end-to-end.\n4. **Phase 4** (0.5 day): wire into `cli/main.py`, Makefile target, .gitignore. Smoke: `make verify-sync` still EXIT=0.\n5. **Phase 5** (1-2 weeks): implement E1-E15 in 3-5 PR batches.\n\n**Rationale for this ordering:** Phase 1 is the riskiest (PTY mechanics). Validating the harness can spawn-and-observe ONE Claude Code session is the gate that determines whether the entire approach is viable. If Phase 1 fails (e.g., Claude Code's TTY behavior breaks pexpect-style detection), we re-pivot before committing to the rest of the architecture.\n\n---\n\n## Sign-off\n\n| Decision | Status | Signed | Date |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| D-1: PTY layer = fork ptytest | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n| D-2: Assertion DSL = Expect.* port | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n| D-3: HOME isolation = compose IsolationLayers | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n| D-4: Eval registry = YAML + callback escape | 🟡 PROPOSED | — | — |\n| D-5: Hook-matcher coverage gate (G5 falsifiable) | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n| D-6: `--max-disk-mb` poller (R4 enforcement) | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n| D-7: Three-layer path-traversal hardening | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n| D-8: Reporter consumes N' + status taxonomy | 🟡 PROPOSED (R2) | — | — |\n\n**Maintainer:** mark each row 🟢 APPROVED + sign + date before invoking `/sc:roadmap`.\n\n**Reject/revise:** add a row to a \"Revisions\" section below; do not edit the original decisions in-place.\n\n---\n\n## Revisions\n\n(none yet)\n\n---\n\n## D-9: Reconcile `validation.status: \"fail\"` label and document manual-triage policy for unclassified deviations\n\n### Context\n\n`.roadmap-state.json:113-117` reports `fidelity_status: \"pass\"` and `validation.status: \"fail\"` (timestamp 2026-05-18T19:47:24Z, six minutes after the final pipeline step completed). However, every pipeline step in `steps.*` — including `spec-fidelity`, `wiring-verification`, `deviation-analysis`, and `remediate` — recorded `status: \"PASS\"`. `spec-fidelity.md:1-7` likewise records 0 HIGH/MEDIUM/LOW deviations and PASS convergence.\n\nThe `validation.status: \"fail\"` label was stamped by a separate validator step that reacts to the 20 records in `spec-deviations.md`. Per `spec-deviations.md:10`, \"deviation classification is not yet implemented. All records currently render as UNCLASSIFIED.\" `remediation-tasklist.md:14-34` then escalates all 20 to `BLOCKING` with owner `UNKNOWN` purely because the classifier never ran — not because spec-fidelity actually regressed.\n\nA manual cross-check (performed during this reconciliation) confirms:\n\n- All 15 spec→roadmap \"orphan\" findings (4 files + 11 `Expect.*` predicate helpers) ARE present in `roadmap.md` — the files appear at `roadmap.md:102,142,409-412` and the 11 helpers are enumerated in row `COMP-010` at `roadmap.md:77`. The analyzer's bare-identifier string match missed qualified references (e.g., `Expect.jsonl.contains_event`) and tabular row contents.\n- All 5 roadmap→spec \"orphan\" IDs (`D-1`, `D12`, `D3`, `D5`, `D6`) are NOT present in `roadmap.md` as bare tokens at all. Only `D-5` and `D-8` (ADR labels) appear (`roadmap.md:57,86,109,344,348`). These are phantom matches from a buggy extraction regex, not real roadmap references.\n\nNet: 20/20 deviations are false-positives produced by the unimplemented classifier. Spec-fidelity is genuinely PASS.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Pros | Cons |\n|---|---|---|\n| **A. Add `validation.unclassified_deviations: 20` and `validation.status: \"pass-with-triage\"`; document manual-triage policy here; produce `deviation-triage.md`; resolve `remediation-tasklist.md` per disposition.** | Preserves audit trail. Distinguishes \"fidelity failed\" from \"classifier unimplemented, manual triage performed.\" Unblocks Phase 1 execution. | Requires per-deviation manual evidence (done in `deviation-triage.md`). |\n| B. Flip `validation.status` to `\"pass\"` and delete the deviation records. | Cleanest. | Destroys audit trail of what the analyzer flagged and why the maintainer decided each was a false-positive. |\n| C. Leave the label as `\"fail\"` and block Phase 1 until the classifier is implemented (backlog item `pipeline-classifier-implementation`). | Lets the pipeline self-heal eventually. | Stalls the cliEval release on infrastructure the release itself doesn't need. The classifier is a separate roadmap concern. |\n\n### Decision: **A — pass-with-triage label, manual-triage policy, per-deviation disposition.**\n\n### Rationale\n\n1. The pipeline's 14 step statuses already provide an objective PASS signal; the post-pipeline validator's `\"fail\"` label is one composite indicator that conflates two distinct conditions (fidelity failure vs. unclassified records pending triage). Splitting them removes ambiguity for future readers.\n2. Option B destroys the only artifact (`spec-deviations.md` + `deviation-registry.json`) that would let a future classifier replay the same input and verify it produces the same dispositions. Triage is durable; deletion is not.\n3. Option C blocks an unrelated release on a tooling backlog item with no committed timeline. The cliEval release does not depend on classifier implementation — it only depends on the dispositions being recorded.\n4. Manual-triage policy fits within ADR-lite scope: per-deviation rows with file:line evidence in `deviation-triage.md` are auditable, and `remediation-tasklist.md` updates close the loop with the original BLOCKING entries.\n\n### Manual-Triage Policy\n\nWhen `validation.status` is `\"fail\"` purely due to unclassified-classifier output AND `fidelity_status` is `\"pass\"` AND every pipeline `steps.*` is PASS, a maintainer MAY perform manual triage by:\n\n1. Reading each finding in `spec-deviations.md` plus its registry entry in `deviation-registry.json`.\n2. Cross-referencing the cited symbol/file/ID against `roadmap.md` and `decisions.md` to confirm presence or absence.\n3. Assigning one of the following dispositions:\n   - `NO_ACTION` — finding is a false-positive (analyzer extraction artifact or naming-only mismatch); cite the roadmap.md line that contains the symbol.\n   - `MAPPED → <ROADMAP_ROW_ID>` — finding maps to an existing roadmap row; cite the row.\n   - `ADD_TO_P1` / `ADD_NEW_TASK` — genuine gap; do NOT silently edit phase tasklists; surface for separate gated decision.\n   - `BLOCKING-CONFIRMED` — actual fidelity failure; halts the release.\n4. Recording per-deviation dispositions in `deviation-triage.md` with file:line evidence.\n5. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Qy93tRHVa6VDDA7XQQjfSK","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# D-0015 — implementation notes\n2\t\n3\t## Decisions made during build\n4\t\n5\t1. **Why `uv pip list` instead of parsing `pyproject.toml`.** AC3 names\n6\t   `pexpect` explicitly even though it is purely transitive (via vendored\n7\t   `ptytest`). Parsing `[project.dependencies]` would never see `pexpect`\n8\t   because it is not a direct dep, so the check would silently pass even if\n9\t   ptytest brought in pexpect ≥ N versions. `uv pip list` captures the\n10\t   actual installed surface, which is what AC3 is constraining.\n11\t\n12\t2. **Allow-list as a committed file (`scripts/dependency_baseline.txt`)\n13\t   rather than a Python constant.** The list moves through PR review when a\n14\t   real dep change happens. Putting it in a Python constant would conflate\n15\t   \"policy data\" with \"policy logic\" — the spec wants the data to be the\n16\t   diffable artefact.\n17\t\n18\t3. **Name-only comparison (versions ignored).** Pinning versions in the\n19\t   baseline would mean every minor `pytest` bump fails CI, which is noise.\n20\t   AC3 is a *boundary* check, not a *lockfile*; the dependency-lock concern\n21\t   is a separate, future-roadmap item.\n22\t\n23\t4. **PEP 503 normalisation.** Both `uv pip list` and source-of-truth lists\n24\t   can drift on `_` vs `-` and casing. Normalising (`lower()` +\n25\t   `replace('_','-')`) makes the check stable across upstream metadata\n26\t   renames.\n27\t\n28\t5. **Removals do not fail.** Allow-list is treated as an upper bound, not an\n29\t   equality check. A dev tool can be dropped without ceremony; only\n30\t   additions are the AC3 concern.\n31\t\n32\t6. **`ptyprocess` pre-added to allow-list.** `pexpect` requires `ptyprocess`\n33\t   at runtime, so AC3's explicit `pexpect` allowance implicitly carries\n34\t   `ptyprocess`. Pre-adding it avoids a CI surprise when the ptytest vendor\n35\t   drop lands in Phase 2.\n36\t\n37\t7. **`superclaude` itself in the baseline.** The editable install registers\n38\t   `superclaude` as a top-level package, so it appears in `uv pip list`. Parsing `[project.dependencies]` would never see `pexpect`\n   because it is not a direct dep, so the check would silently pass even if\n   ptytest brought in pexpect ≥ N versions. `uv pip list` captures the\n   actual installed surface, which is what AC3 is constraining.\n\n2. **Allow-list as a committed file (`scripts/dependency_baseline.txt`)\n   rather than a Python constant.** The list moves through PR review when a\n   real dep change happens. Putting it in a Python constant would conflate\n   \"policy data\" with \"policy logic\" — the spec wants the data to be the\n   diffable artefact.\n\n3. **Name-only comparison (versions ignored).** Pinning versions in the\n   baseline would mean every minor `pytest` bump fails CI, which is noise.\n   AC3 is a *boundary* check, not a *lockfile*; the dependency-lock concern\n   is a separate, future-roadmap item.\n\n4. **PEP 503 normalisation.** Both `uv pip list` and source-of-truth lists\n   can drift on `_` vs `-` and casing. Normalising (`lower()` +\n   `replace('_','-')`) makes the check stable across upstream metadata\n   renames.\n\n5. **Removals do not fail.** Allow-list is treated as an upper bound, not an\n   equality check. A dev tool can be dropped without ceremony; only\n   additions are the AC3 concern.\n\n6. **`ptyprocess` pre-added to allow-list.** `pexpect` requires `ptyprocess`\n   at runtime, so AC3's explicit `pexpect` allowance implicitly carries\n   `ptyprocess`. Pre-adding it avoids a CI surprise when the ptytest vendor\n   drop lands in Phase 2.\n\n7. **`superclaude` itself in the baseline.** The editable install registers\n   `superclaude` as a top-level package, so it appears in `uv pip list`. It\n   stays in the baseline so the check passes today.\n","numLines":40,"startLine":1,"totalLines":99}}}
